And so, though with their bodies they labour in this world, their outward actions disguise their inward transcendence of higher realities: they act, but they are not bound by the fruits of their action.

Such souls have not merely understood this principle; they have been seized by it, existentially, at the very root of their being. It is the principle voiced in the Qur'anic verse, 'And the life of this world is naught but sport and play; and truly, the abode of the Hereafter—that is true life' (29:64). Their instincts, therefore, are always attuned to that higher reality. When Ali (ع) was struck by the poisoned sword of Ibn Muljam, the cry that broke from him was not one of pain but of triumph: 'I have triumphed, by the Lord of the Ka'ba!' At the very moment of death there was in him nothing but the remembrance of God, and the Imam passed away, it is said, with his tongue still repeating the words la ilaha illa'Llah. His death was thus consummated exactly as his life had been lived, according to the wisdom of the Prophet, who, when he was asked which of all acts is the most meritorious, replied: 'That you die while your tongue is moistened with the invocation of God.'

All the rites and practices associated with the chivalric orders of Islam were traced, without exception, back to the Prophet's son-in-law, Ali (ع) ibn Abi Talib, who was thereby established as the supreme source of the virtues of futuwwa — so Muhammad Ja'far Mahjub observes in his 'Chivalry and Early Persian Sufism' (in Lewisohn, ed., Heritage, vol. 1, p. 552). S.H. Nasr writes in the same vein that Ali (ع), having received the 'truth and power' of futuwwa from the Prophet, 'became the supreme source of futuwwa in Islam for both Sunnis and Shi'ites... The personality of Ali, at once sage and knight, contemplative and protector of laborers and craftsmen, has continued to dominate through the centuries over the horizon of futuwwa as it has over much of Sufism' ('Spiritual Chivalry', in Nasr, ed., Islamic Spirituality, vol. 2, pp. 305–306). Nasr draws attention as well to the organic relationship between futuwwa and the guilds — the asnaf, singular sinf — of craftsmen and artists that were established throughout the length and breadth of the Muslim world.

It is often said that the Naqshabandi tariqa is the one exception to this rule, tracing its descent from the Prophet through the first caliph, Abu Bakr, rather than through Ali (ع). But this affiliation with Abu Bakr pertains only to one of its three principal silsilas. In his al-Hada'iq al-wardiyya fi haqa'iq ajilla al-Naqshabandiyya (Damascus, 1306/1889, p. 6), 'Abd al-Majid b. Muhammad al-Khani — himself a Naqshabandi-Khalidi shaykh — calls the first chain (al-silsilat al-ula) the 'golden chain', and this begins with Ali (ع), proceeding through the Shi'i Imams down to Ali (ع) b. Musa, then to Ma'ruf al-Karkhi and the other masters. The second chain (al-silsilatu'l-thaniya) likewise begins with Ali (ع), proceeding through Hasan al-Basri. Only the third chain, which is called al-silsilat al-siddiqiyya, proceeds from the Prophet to Abu Bakr (al-siddiq) — a reference owed to Professor Mahmut Kilic.

## The First Sermon of Nahj al-Balagha

This, the opening sermon of ⟪ar:نھج البلاغہ⟫, Maulana Ali (AS) delivered as a ⟪ar:خطبۃ⟫ proclaiming the oneness of God—a oneness, he teaches, that cannot fall into the category of numbers. It is built upon paradox, for the loftier the truth he means to reveal, the deeper the paradox he forms to reveal it: by negating the reach of human words and concepts he affirms the Reality that no word and no concept can bound. This is the manner of his sermons—the eye cannot see Him, yet the mind cannot deny Him; He is farther than the flights of imagination can reach, yet nearer to us than our own jugular vein—and nowhere is it plainer than in these first lines. Praise be to God—الحَمْدُ للهِ الَّذَي لاَ يَبْلُغُ مِدْحَتَهُ القَائِلُونَ—whose laudation those who speak cannot deliver; here is the very paradox of speech, whose office is to deliver a concept, turned to show that God is so lofty and sublime that words can deliver nothing of Him. Whose graces those who count cannot number—وَلاِ يُحْصِي نَعْمَاءَهُ العَادُّونَ—for counting is for enumeration, but not for His bounties; whose rightful due those who strive cannot render—ولاَ يُؤَدِّي حَقَّهُ الُمجْتَهِدُونَ—for however hard they strive they can never render Him His due of praise and gratitude. He who cannot be grasped by far-reaching aspirations—الَّذِي لاَ يُدْركُهُ بُعْدُ الهِمَمِ—nor fathomed by profound intuitions—وَلاَ يَنَالُهُ غَوْصُ الفِطَنِ; He whose attribute has no binding limitation, no existing description, no time appointed, no term extended—الَّذِي لَيْسَ لِصِفَتِهِ حَدٌّ مَحْدُودٌ، وَلاَ نَعْتٌ مَوْجُودٌ، وَلا وَقْتٌ مَعْدُودٌ—so that His attribute cannot be confined to a definition and yet is affirmed as real, described by the very words of the revelation even though no description can capture Him. Through His power He originated all created things, and through His mercy He diffused the comforting winds; and He entrenched with rocky pillars His swaying earth.

Foremost in religion is knowledge of Him, and the perfection of this knowledge is believing in Him, and the perfection of this belief is affirming His oneness, and the perfection of this affirmation is to purify one's devotion to Him—the perfection of sincerity, أخلاص, which is to render one's devotion and one's very conception of God pure—and the perfection of this purification is to divest Him of all attributes (نفی الصفۃ), because of the testimony of every attribute that it is other than the object of attribution, and because of the testimony of every such object that it is other than the attribute. So whoever ascribes an attribute to God—glorified be He!—has conjoined Him [with something else], and whoever so conjoins Him has made Him twofold, and whoever makes Him twofold has fragmented Him, and whoever thus fragments Him is ignorant of Him. And whoever points to Him confines Him, and whoever confines Him counts Him; and whoever asks 'in what?' encloses Him, and whoever asks 'upon what?' isolates Him. For the very attributes by which He is named in the revelation—that He is the All-Compassionate (رحمن), the All-Merciful (رحیم), the Almighty (عزیز)—are affirmed as real and yet cannot be defined, bounded, or described: He is merciful, but not as any mercy we can picture; powerful, but not as any storm or flood or force we have known. Reason itself 'de-fines,' drawing a boundary, a ⟪ar:حد⟫, around whatever it grasps, and by breaking that boundary the sermon opens an awareness beyond the reach of speculative thought, so that in negating the limits of human words it affirms the Absolute that has no limit. This is the very movement of the testimony of faith: لا الہ, 'no deity,' which is the negation (نفی), and الا اللہ, 'except God,' which is the affirmation (اثبات); each attribute is negated only insofar as the limited mind conceives it, and is thereby affirmed as nothing but God Himself—La Rahmana illa'Llah, there is no All-Compassionate but God; La Raheema illa'Llah, there is no All-Merciful but God. So is God glorified above all that the mind would attribute to Him: ‏سُبْحَنَ ٱللَّهِ عَمَّا يَصِفُونَ—'Glorified is God above all that they attribute to Him!' (Surat al-Mu'minun, 23:91).

Being, but not by way of any becoming; existing, but not from having been non-existent; with every thing, but not through association; and other than every thing, but not through separation; acting, but not through movements and instruments; seeing, even when nothing of His creation was to be seen; solitary, even when there was none whose intimacy might be sought or whose absence might be missed.

He brought forth creation, originating it from the very beginning, without pondering over any deliberation or benefiting from any [previous] experience; without initiating any movement or engaging in any unsettling exertion. He assigned to all things their span of time and harmonized their differences; He instilled within them their natural dispositions, impressing upon them their respective forms; knowing them before their genesis, encompassing their boundaries and their finalities; aware of their configurations and their contours.

Then He—glorified be He!—created a fissure in the atmospheres, cleaving up directions and the uppermost levels of air; then He caused water to flow therein, a current of surging waves, swelling and swirling in overflowing abundance. Then He mounted it upon a tempestuous wind, a thunderous gale; then He made the wind prevent the return of the water, enabling it to dominate the violence of its flow, and aligning it with the boundaries of the water; the air beneath the wind was spread wide open, while the water above it flowed copiously.

Then He raised up a wind, making it sterile, fixing its course, intensifying its current, distancing it from its source. Then he ordered it to agitate the swelling waters and to stir up oceanic waves. The wind churned up the waters—as one churns up milk in a milkbag—imparting to the water its own violence in the atmosphere, turning its foremost back upon its hindmost, and its stillness to its motion, so that its torrents surged and its swelling waves threw up foam. Then He elevated [the foam] within a cleft air and a vast open atmosphere and formed from it the seven heavens, making the lowest of them a suspended wave and the highest a protected shelter and an elevated ceiling, without any pillars to support them or any clinch to transfix them. Then He adorned the heavens with the ornaments of the stars and the effulgence of the firmament; and made a radiant lamp [the sun] and an illuminating moon to follow an orbit, in a revolving sphere, [beneath] a roof in motion and a firmament in flux. Then He split up the highest heavens and filled them with angels of different dispositions: some prostrating and not bowing; others bowing and not standing upright; some arrayed in ranks, not separating from each other; others glorifying, never wearying—not overcome by sleepiness in the eyes, distraction in the mind, fatigue of the body or the negligence of forgetfulness. And among them are custodians of His Revelation, and conveyors [of His Words] to His Messengers, and those who come and go with His Decree and His Command. And among them are the guardians of His slaves, and the wardens of the gates of His Gardens. And among them are those whose feet stand firmly on the lowest worlds, and whose necks surpass the highest heaven, and whose sides exceed all bounds, and whose shoulders are proportioned to the pillars of the Throne, their eyes cast down before it, enwrapping themselves in their wings beneath it. Between them and all others there is a barrier: veils of Glory and screens of Might. They do not imagine their Lord through formal representation, nor ascribe to Him created attributes; they do not confine Him within places, nor do they allude to Him through similitudes.

Then He—glorified be He!—gathered up a quantity of dust, from some hard earth and some soft, some sweet earth and some salty, and poured water onto it until it was pure, then kneaded into it some moisture until it stuck together; then he fashioned from this a form with curves and joints, limbs and parts. He compacted it until it held tightly together, solidified it until it became as sounding clay, assigning to it a measured duration and a known consummation. Then He breathed into it of His spirit, and it stood up as a human being, endowed with intellectual powers with which to reflect, thoughts by which to conduct himself, limbs to put to service, instruments at his disposal, and knowledge with which to discern between the true and the false, between different tastes and fragrances, and between different colours and categories; having a constitution compounded of different hues, unified forms, contrasting oppositions, and distinct admixtures of heat and cold, moisture and dryness.

And God—glorified be He!—demanded from the angels the fulfilment of that with which He had charged them, and their compliance with His injunction to them, by [ordering] their submissive prostration to him [Adam] and humble reverence of him. So He said, 'Prostrate to Adam', and they prostrated, except Iblis. Passionate scorn deranged him, wretchedness overwhelmed him; he prided himself upon [his] constitution of fire and disdained the creature made of clay. Then God granted him a respite, that His wrath be fully warranted, that the affliction be consummated, and that the promise be fulfilled. So He said, 'Thou art of those given respite unto a day whose time is appointed'.

Then He—glorified be He!—settled Adam in an abode wherein He made his life comfortable, and his domain secure; and He warned him of Iblis and his enmity. But his enemy beguiled him, through envious desire for his immortal abode and his intimacy with the righteous. So Adam exchanged certainty for his doubt and resolution for his weakness; and replaced joy with dread and self-esteem with remorse. Then God accepted his repentance, and whelmed him with a word from His Mercy and promised him a return to His Garden. Then He made him descend to the abode of tribulations and to the begetting of progeny.

And from his offspring He chose prophets, taking from them a pledge in respect of the Revelation, entrusting to them the conveyance of His Message; this, at a time when most of His creatures had deviated from their covenant with God, so that they ignored His due and set up peers alongside Him, and the demons distracted them from knowledge of Him and sundered them from worship of Him. So He sent amongst them His messengers, dispatching prophets in succession, in order to claim from them [His creatures] the fulfilment of the covenant of His creation; to remind them of His forgotten graces; to remonstrate with them through communication [of His Revelation]; to unearth for them the buried treasures of the intellects; and to show them signs of omnipotence: the outspread earth laid low beneath them, provisions which give them life, finalities which mete out death; ailments hastening their decrepitude, and a succession of events that flow over them.

And God never deprived His creation of a prophet sent forth, a revealed scripture, an imperative proof or a clear path; messengers who were not diminished by the paucity of their number, nor by the multitude of their deniers. Amongst them, those who came before were told the names of those who would follow them, and those who came after were informed of those who came before. Thus, generations were begotten and ages elapsed, fathers passing away and sons succeeding them, until God sent forth Muhammad, Messenger of God, in fulfilment of His promise and completion of His [cycle of] prophecy; he regarding whom a pledge was taken from the prophets, renowned through signs, of noble birth.

At this time the people on earth were split up into different religious communities; caprice was widespread, pathways were disordered: among them [such paths as] likening God to His creation, distorting His Name, alluding to other than Him. So God guided them through him [the Prophet] out of error and, through his presence, delivered them from their ignorance.

Then He chose for Muhammad the meeting with Him and desired for him what was with Himself, and removed him with dignity from the abode of the lower world and took him away from any link with [the domain of] trial; so He drew him to Himself honourably. He left behind amongst you that which the prophets leave for their communities, lest they abandon them to stray as they will, without a clear path and a visible beacon: the scripture of your Lord, [the Prophet] clarifying what is lawful and unlawful according to it, the obligatory and the recommended, [verses that] abrogate and [others that are] abrogated therein, the conditionally permissible and the unconditionally compulsory, the specific and the general, His admonitions and analogies, undelimited and delimited [ordinances], unambiguous and equivocal [verses]; elucidating what is condense and clarifying what is obscure therein.

In it are things the knowledge of which is binding upon His slaves, and others, ignorance of which is overlooked; there are injunctions affirmed as obligatory in the scripture yet known to have been abrogated by the Sunna, and actions made obligatory by the Sunna from which the scripture has nonetheless permitted abstention; there are duties incumbent upon a person at their prescribed time which then cease to be incumbent once that time has passed. In it, too, is drawn the distinction between the forbidden acts: the major ones, which He has threatened to punish with His fires, and the minor ones, for which He has prepared His forgiveness. And in it are described acts that are acceptable when performed to a modest extent and acceptable again when performed to a higher degree, without either being made obligatory.

## The Letter of Ali (ع) to Malik al-Ashtar

This letter was written when the Imam appointed Malik as governor of Egypt and its regions, at a time when the rule of its earlier governor, Muhammad b. Abi Bakr, had become unstable. It is the longest of the letters the Imam wrote, and the most all-embracing in beauty of form and excellence of meaning.

In the Name of God, the Compassionate, the Merciful.

This is what the servant of God, Ali (ع), Commander of the Faithful, commands Malik b. al-Harith al-Ashtar in his mandate ('ahd) of appointment, on making him governor of Egypt: to collect its revenues, to fight its enemies, to seek the welfare of its people, and to bring prosperity to its lands.

He commands him to fear God, to prefer obedience to God above all things, and to abide by what He has commanded in His Book—acts both obligatory and recommended—for no one attains virtue except by following them, and no one becomes wretched except by rejecting and neglecting them. He commands him to assist God with his heart, his hand, and his tongue, for God—majestic is His Name—has undertaken to grant victory to whoever assists Him and to elevate whoever exalts Him. And he commands him to break the passions of his soul and to restrain it when whim and caprice run high, for the soul incites to evil, unless God has mercy.

Know, Malik, that I am sending you to a land that has been ruled by governments before you, some just and some unjust, and that the people will now weigh your conduct just as you once weighed the conduct of the rulers before you. They will speak of you just as you spoke of them; and the righteous are known only through what God causes to flow about them on the tongues of His servants. So let the treasure most beloved to you be the treasure of good deeds. Master your desires, and hold yourself back from what is not lawful for you, for such self-restraint is fairness by the soul towards itself, keeping it balanced between what it likes and what it dislikes.

Habituate your heart to mercy, love, and kindness towards your subjects. Do not stand over them like a ravenous beast that counts it fair game to devour them, for they are of two kinds: either your brother in religion or your like in creation. Mistakes slip from them and faults befall them, whether deliberately or by accident. So extend to them your forgiveness and your pardon, in the same way you would wish God to extend to you His forgiveness and pardon; for you are above them, and the one in command over you is above you, and God is above the one who appointed you. He has asked you to manage their affairs, and He is testing you through them.

Do not set your soul up for war with God, for before His retribution you have no power, and of His forgiveness and mercy you cannot do without. Never regret a pardon, and never rejoice in a punishment. Do not act rashly on any impulse when you can find a way around it. Do not say, "I have been given authority, so I command and am obeyed," for that breeds corruption in the heart, weakens religion, and draws the turns of fortune near. If the authority you hold gives rise to pride or vanity in you, then look at the greatness of God's dominion above you, and at His power over you—power the like of which you do not hold even over your own self. This will calm your ambition, restrain your vehemence, and restore to you the good sense that had strayed from you. Beware of matching yourself with God in greatness or likening yourself to Him in might, for God humbles every tyrant and disgraces every braggart.

Be just with God and be just with people, giving them their due from yourself, from your close relatives, and from those of your subjects for whom you have any fondness—for if you do not, you are an oppressor. And whoever oppresses the servants of God has God, rather than His servants, for his adversary; and whomever God opposes, He refutes his plea, and he remains at war with God until he desists and repents. Nothing so surely hastens the withdrawal of God's grace and the coming of His retribution as persistence in oppression, for God hears the cry of the oppressed, and He lies in wait for the oppressors.

Let the dearest of your affairs be those most balanced in truth, most comprehensive in justice, and most inclusive of your subjects' contentment—for the discontent of the common folk (al-'amma) wipes out the contentment of the elite (al-khassa), while the discontent of the elite is forgiven amid the contentment of the common folk. No one is more of a burden to the ruler in times of ease, less helpful in times of hardship, more averse to fair treatment, more insistent in asking favours, less grateful when given them, slower to accept an excuse when refused, and weaker in patience under the blows of fortune, than the elite. It is the common folk of the community who are the pillar of religion, the mass of the Muslims, and the defence against enemies—so let your inclination be towards them, and your leaning be with them.

Let those of your subjects who most eagerly hunt out the faults of others be the ones furthest from you and the most despised in your eyes, for people do have faults, and the ruler is the person most bound to conceal them. So do not lay bare what is hidden from you; your duty is only to correct what has become plain to you, and God will judge whatever remains hidden from you. Veil people's failings as much as you can, and God will veil from your subjects the failings in yourself that you would wish concealed. Loosen from the people every knot of resentment, and cut away from your own heart the cord of every enmity. Turn a blind eye to whatever is not clear to you, and do not be quick to believe a slanderer, for a slanderer is a cheat, even when he appears in the guise of a sincere adviser.

Do not admit into your counsel a miser, for he will turn you away from generosity and frighten you with poverty; nor a coward, for he will make you feel too weak for your affairs; nor a greedy man, for he will dress up avidity for you as though it were justice. For miserliness, cowardice, and greed are separate traits gathered together by one bad opinion of God. The worst of your ministers is the one who was a minister to evil rulers before you and shared in their sins, so do not let such men into your inner circle, for they are helpers of sinners and brothers of tyrants. You can find good substitutes for them among men of the same intelligence and capability, but who carry none of their burden of sin and have never helped a tyrant in his tyranny nor a sinner in his sin. Such men will cost you the least, support you the best, incline to you most in affection, and attach themselves least to anyone but you—so choose them as your intimates, in private and in public.

Among these, give the highest place to the one who most readily speaks the bitter truth to you and gives you the least backing in whatever you do that God dislikes for His friends, however painfully it strikes at your own desires. Attach yourself to people of piety and truthfulness, and train them not to flatter you or gladden you with praise for something you never did, for excessive praise breeds conceit and carries a man towards vanity. Never place the good and the wicked on the same footing before you, for that would make the good think little of their goodness and the wicked settle deeper into their wickedness; hold each of them to what he has bound himself to. Know that nothing so builds a ruler's confidence in his subjects as his kindness towards them, his easing of their hardships, and his refusal to press them for what is beyond their power. So conduct yourself in a way that earns you confidence in your subjects, for such confidence will spare you long trouble; and the one most deserving of your good opinion is the one you have treated well, while the one most deserving of your distrust is the one you have treated badly.

Do not break any good tradition that the leaders of this community established, by which harmony was knit together and the subjects were set right; and do not bring in any new practice that harms the good ones already in place—for then the reward for the traditions will go to the one who founded them, while the burden of their breaking will fall on you. Study much with the scholars (al-'ulama) and hold much discourse with the sages (al-hukama), so as to strengthen what keeps your lands sound and to uphold what those before you had already set right.

Know that the subjects are of several classes, none of which can be sound without the others, and none of which can do without the rest. Among them are the soldiers of God (junud Allah); the scribes (kuttab, sing. katib) who serve the common people and the elite; the judges who administer justice (qudat al-'adl); the officials who uphold fairness and secure the right of redress (rifq); those who pay the poll-tax (jizya, ⟪ar:جزیۃ⟫) and the land-tax (kharaj, ⟪ar:خَرَج⟫) from among the protected people (ahl al-dhimma) and the Muslims; the merchants and artisans; and the lowest class, the needy and the destitute. To each of these God has assigned its share, and He has fixed its bounds and its duties in His Book or in the Sunna (⟪ar:سنۃ⟫) of His Prophet—God bless him and his family—as a binding covenant ('ahd) from Him, kept safe with us.

So as regards the soldiers, they are—by the grace of God—the fortresses of the subjects, the adornment of the governors, the power of religion, and the pathways to security (subul al-amn). The subjects cannot maintain themselves except through the soldiers, and the soldiers in their turn cannot be maintained except through the kharaj—the revenues God brings out for them—by which they gain the strength to wage war on their enemies, on which they depend for their own welfare, and by which they meet their needs. Neither of these two classes can be maintained except through the third: the judges, the administrators and the scribes, who secure every contract, gather and order all interests and benefits, and are entrusted with the special and the general affairs of state alike. None of these can function without the merchants and the artisans, who gather the goods that are needed and set up the markets, and who by their own labour supply what could not be procured by the work of others. Then, last, comes the lowest class, the needy and the destitute, whose relief and assistance is a duty owed to them. For every one of these classes there is plenitude with God, and every one has upon the governor a right measured by what its welfare requires. And the governor cannot fully discharge the duties God has laid upon him except through earnest striving and seeking God's help (al-ihtimam wa'l-isti'ana), schooling himself to hold fast to what is right, and bearing patiently whatever comes to him, be it easy or hard.

Appoint as commander of your soldiers the man you feel most deeply to be the most sincere toward God, the Prophet and your Imam, the purest of heart, and the most excellent in forbearance (hilm)—one who is slow to anger, glad to pardon, kind to the weak and firm with the strong, neither driven by violence nor held back by weakness. Hold fast to men of noble descent, from pious families of established name and repute, and to those known for their bravery, their courage, their generosity (al-sakha') and their tolerance (al-samaha)—for they are a group formed by nobility and a party made of honour. Then supervise their affairs as parents would supervise their child. Let no act by which you strengthen them appear too great in your eyes, and do not belittle any kindness, however slight, which you have promised them, for such kindness is as a summoner (da'iya) unto them, calling them to give you good counsel and to place their trust in you. Do not abandon the close inspection of their affairs for the sake of matters that seem weightier, for there is a case in which they benefit from even a small act of kindness, and a case in which they cannot do without the weighty matters.

Let the most preferred of your commanders be the one most benevolent to the people and most generous to them with his bounty, so that they have ample means—they and those among their relatives who succeed them. Let their concern be gathered into one resolve: to fight the enemy. Truly, your kindness toward them will cause their hearts to incline toward you.

The greatest source of joy for governors is the establishment of justice in the land and the flowing of love from the subjects. Such love will not appear unless their hearts are secure, and their counsel will not be sound except through the governor's oversight of those charged with the subjects' affairs, and through his ensuring that they find the burden of rule light and easy to bear, so that they no longer anxiously hope for the governor's rule to end. So raise their hopes, keep up your continuous praise of them, and recount the brave deeds of those who have been brave—for truly, abundant praise of noble acts rouses the brave and spurs on the lazy, if God wills. Acknowledge the courageous deed of every man, and do not ascribe the deed of one to another, nor fall short in granting anyone his due. Do not let the nobility of a man lead you to reckon a small accomplishment great, and do not let the lowliness of a man lead you to reckon a great one small. Refer to God and His Messenger any matter that weighs heavily upon you or that is unclear to you, for God the Exalted has said to a folk whom He loved to guide: 'O ye who believe, obey God and obey the Messenger and those in authority among you. And if ye dispute with one another over anything, then refer it to God and the Messenger' (Qur'an, 4: 59). To 'refer to God' means to follow that which is clear and unequivocal in His Book; and to 'refer to the Messenger' means to follow that part of his Sunna which unites rather than that which divides.

For the settlement of disputes among the people, choose the one you hold to be the most excellent of your subjects—one whom complex matters do not confound and whom disputants do not enrage; who does not persist in a fault, and does not shrink from returning to the truth once he perceives it; whose soul is not drawn toward greed; who is not content with a shallow understanding but probes to its depths; who is most circumspect in the face of ambiguities, most firm in holding to the proofs, least wearied by the litigants' returning again and again, most patient in uncovering the true state of affairs, and most resolute once the right judgement is plain; whom praise does not make vain and whom enticement does not sway. Such people are indeed rare.

Then examine carefully and often the carrying-out of the judge's verdicts, and be generous in what you pay him, so that any want is removed and his need to seek help from the people is lessened. Give him a rank of nearness to yourself that none of your other close companions might hope to attain, so that he is safe in your presence and shielded from the attacks of other influential men. Scrutinize all of this with a piercing scrutiny, for this religion had been held captive in the hands of evil people, abused by them for their own caprice and exploited by them for the sake of this world.

Then look into the affairs of your administrators. Employ them only after testing them, and do not appoint them out of any partiality or favouritism, for these two are a wellspring of every kind of injustice and treachery. Seek out from among them the men of proven experience and modesty, from righteous households and families of precedence in Islam, for they are the most noble in character, the most rooted in dignity, the least inclined toward greed, and the most mindful of how all things end. Then provide them with a generous livelihood, for this gives them the strength to keep themselves in good order, frees them from reaching for the funds in their charge, and stands as an argument against them should they disobey your orders or betray your trust. Examine their actions carefully by appointing over them supervisors known for their sincerity and reliability, for your quiet watching of their affairs will move them to keep faith with their trust and to be gentle with the subjects. And keep watch over your assistants: if any one of them stretches out his hand to a treacherous deed, and the reports of your watchers agree upon it so that you hold it sufficient evidence, then lay corporal punishment upon him, and take him to account for what his act has brought about. Set him in a place of disgrace, brand him with the charge of treachery, and gird him with the shame of the accusation.

Look, too, into the matter of the kharaj land-tax in such a way that those subject to it are kept in a proper state of well-being, for it is from their welfare, and from the sound collection of the tax itself, that the good of all the others is drawn; there can be no welfare of the rest except through them, since all without exception depend upon the revenues of the kharaj and upon those who pay it. Let your concern for the cultivation of the land outweigh your concern for the collection of the tax, for no tax will be gathered if there is no cultivation. Whoever exacts the tax without cultivating the land ruins the land and destroys the people, and his rule will not endure for long. So if they complain of a heavy burden, or of some blight, or of the cutting off of irrigation, or of a lack of rain, or of a change in the condition of the soil from flooding or from drought, then lighten their burden, in the measure that you hope will set their situation right. And do not let any relief you grant them by lightening their burden weigh heavily upon you, for it is an investment that will return to you in the prosperity of your land and the adornment of your rule, in the finest praise you reap from them, and in the pride you take in dispensing justice among them. You may then rely upon an increase in their capacity as a result of what you invested in them when you gave them respite, and you may trust them, having accustomed them to your justice and your kindness.

It is possible that a situation might arise in which you depend upon them: they would then bear this responsibility gladly, for a prosperous land can bear a burden which you place upon it. The devastation of the land only comes about through the destitution of its inhabitants; and the destitution of its inhabitants only comes about when the desire to amass wealth rules the souls of the governors, when they have doubts about what endures, and when they profit little from exemplary teachings.

Now consider with care the situation of your scribes, appointing the best of them in charge of your affairs. Assign those letters containing the most sensitive strategies and secrets to the scribe in whom you find the most comprehensive moral qualities; one who will not be emboldened by his elevated status to contradict you in the presence of others at a public assembly; one who is not negligent in delivering to you the correspondence of your officials, and properly despatching your replies, regarding what he takes and gives on your behalf. He must not weaken any agreement contracted for you, nor be incapable of repudiating that which has been contracted to your detriment. He must not be ignorant of the value of his own capabilities; for one who is unaware of the value of his own capabilities will be even more ignorant of the value of others. Do not make your selection only on the basis of your own discrimination, confidence and trust, for men know how to present themselves to the discrimination of rulers through affectation and good service, behind which there is neither sincerity nor fidelity. Rather, test them by what they did under the good people who came before you, and appoint the one whose virtue left the deepest impression upon the common people, and whose reputation for integrity is most widely known. This will be proof of your sincerity towards God, and towards the one on whose behalf you have been appointed to this office; for you will be putting the right man in the right place, and in so doing enacting justice.

Appoint for each of your affairs a head-officer among your officials, one who will not be daunted by the magnitude of affairs nor dispersed by their multiplicity. Any fault of your scribes which you have ignored will be ascribed to yourself.

Then attend to the merchants and artisans, treating them well and urging the same on others — those of fixed abode, those who travel with their goods, and those who earn their livelihood with their hands. They are sources of benefit and the means by which conveniences are obtained. By them are procured goods from distant and remote places, brought by land and sea, from plains and mountains, and from places where men do not settle nor even dare to venture. They are in a state of reconciliation (silm), and from them no calamity need be feared; and they are in a state of peace (sulh), from them no disturbance need be feared. Examine their affairs attentively, both those which are close to you and those in all corners of your land. Be aware that, despite what has been said, there is in many of them a despicable stinginess, a repugnant greed, a propensity to hoard goods, and to arbitrarily fix prices. All of this brings about loss for the populace, and is a source of shame for the governors. So prohibit hoarding, for the Messenger of God—God bless him and his family—prohibited it. Let trading be carried out with propriety and fairness, according to prices which do not harm either of the two parties, the buyers or the sellers. So if any one of them succumbs to the temptation to hoard, after you have prohibited it, inflict upon him an exemplary—but not excessive—punishment.

Then—O God, O God!—pay particular attention to the lowest class, those who have no wherewithal, the destitute, the needy, the afflicted, the disabled. Within this class are those who beg, and those whose wretchedness calls out to be alleviated but who do not beg. Be mindful of God in regard to their rights, for He has entrusted these rights to your care. Assign to them a portion from your public treasury (bayt al-mal), and a portion of the produce of what is taken as booty by the Muslims in every region, for those who are furthest have the same rights as those nearest. Upholding the right of each of them is incumbent upon you. Do not let any haughtiness on your part cause you to neglect them, for you will not be pardoned even the slightest shortcoming in fulfilling your obligations towards them as a result of attending to some important matter. So do not turn your concern away from them, nor assume a contemptuous attitude towards them. Keep a watchful eye over the affairs of those who have no access to you, and who are disdained by men of high standing. Appoint from among those you trust a God-fearing and humble person to be responsible for bringing their affairs to your attention. Treat these people in a manner such that God may excuse you on the day you meet Him, for they are more in need of justice from you than any others among your subjects. In regard to each of them, offer your excuse to God in respect of fulfilling his right. Assume responsibility for the orphans and the elderly, those who have no resources yet cannot bring themselves to beg. This is onerous for the governors, and the fulfilment of all rights is onerous, but God makes it light for those who aspire to the Hereafter, who restrain their souls in patience, and trust in the truth of that which is promised them by God.

Apportion a part of your time to those who have special needs, making yourself free to attend to them personally, sitting with them in a public assembly with all due humility before God, your Creator. Keep your soldiers, guards and officers away from them, so that they can speak to you in an uninhibited manner; for I heard the Messenger of God say—God bless him and his family—on more than one occasion: 'A nation in which the rights of the weak are not wrested in an uninhibited manner from the strong will never be blessed.' Bear patiently any coarseness or inarticulate expression that they might manifest; do not show any irritation or disdain towards them—God will thereby extend the most extensive dimensions of His compassion to you and make incumbent the reward for your obedience to Him. Give whatever you give with beneficence, and withhold, if this be unavoidable, with grace and apology.

There are certain affairs which you must take care of personally—among which are replying to your officers if your scribes are incapable of so doing; and issuing responses to the requests of people when they are brought to you in person because your aides find their hearts constricted on their account.

Perform each day the task proper to it, for to each day belongs a particular task. Set apart the most excellent of your available time, and the greatest portions thereof, for your soul, for what is between you and God, even though all times, and the actions performed therein, are for God, if the intention underlying them is good, and if your subjects derive security as a consequence. Let your observance of those duties (fara'id, sing. farida) relating exclusively to God be the special means by which you purify your religion for God. Give unto God of your vital energy in your nights and your days, and perform fully that by which you draw near to God, doing so perfectly, without becoming dull or deficient, taking your body to its limits.

When you lead the people in prayer, do so without repelling them or squandering it, for there are people with infirmities or special needs. Indeed, I asked the Messenger of God—God bless him and his family—when he sent me as his representative to Yemen, 'How should I lead them in prayer?' He replied, 'Lead them according to the prayer performed by the weakest among them; and be merciful to the believers.'

In addition to all of this, do not prolong any period of absence from your subjects, for the isolation of the governors from their subjects is a kind of constriction and causes deficiency in awareness of their affairs. Such isolation cuts rulers off from acquiring knowledge about things hidden from them, so that which is great will appear small, and that which is small, great; the beautiful will appear ugly, and the ugly, beautiful; the true will be mixed with the false. The governor is but a human being: he cannot know what people hide from him. There are no visible signs upon the truth by which apparent expressions of veracity can be distinguished from falsehood.

You can only be one of two types of men: either a man who gives himself generously for the sake of the truth—in which case why seclude yourself, thereby preventing yourself from bestowing a necessary right or performing an honourable deed? Or else you are a man afflicted with refusal to give of oneself—in which case, how quickly people will refrain from making requests of you, despairing of your generosity! This, despite the fact that fulfilling most of the needs which people present to you is not burdensome, whether they be complaints against injustice or demands for fairness in transactions.

The governor has favourites and intimate friends, among whom some are prone to presumptuousness, arrogance and unfairness in transactions. Sever the root of these people by eliminating the causes of these vices, and by not allotting any landed estates to anyone among your entourage or friends. Do not let them entertain any hope that you may grant them some estate; were they to receive such an estate, it would be detrimental to those living adjacent to it, as regards their access to water resources, or any common undertaking, the burden of which would doubtless fall on them, while the profit therefrom would redound to those acquiring the estate, and not upon you. Upon you will fall only the resulting blame ('ayb), both in this world and the next. Impose what is right upon whomsoever it is incumbent, whether he be close to you or distant. Be steadfast and vigilant in this matter, regardless of how it may affect your close ones and favourites. Always desire the consequences of this principle, however heavily it may weigh upon you, for its outcome will be laudable.

If any of your subjects suspect you of an injustice, present your case openly so that you may be cleared of it, turning their suspicions away from you by your plain explanation. Such conduct is a discipline for yourself, a kindness towards your subjects, and a way of pleading your cause that will help you keep them upright in accordance with the truth.

Never reject a call to peace from your enemy when God's pleasure lies in it, for in peace there is rest for your soldiers, relief from your worries, and safety for your lands. But once you have made peace, stay fully on your guard against your enemy, for it may be that he only drew close in order to catch you off guard; so take every precaution, and hold even your own good opinion under suspicion in this matter. Yet if you and your enemy enter into a solemn agreement, or he obtains from you a covenant of protection (dhimma), then keep your agreement faithfully and honour your obligation of protection with all trust. Make your very life a shield for what you have promised, for among all the obligations God has laid down there is none on which people stand more firmly united—despite the differences of their inclinations and the variety of their opinions—than the honouring of pledges. The polytheists themselves observed it among one another, quite apart from their dealings with the Muslims, so greatly did they dread the consequences of treachery. So do not betray your covenant of protection, do not break your promise, and do not deal treacherously with your enemy, for none dares defy God but an ignorant and wretched man (jahil shaqiyy). God has made His pledge and His protection a sanctuary of security, which by His mercy He has spread over His servants—a refuge in whose inviolable strength they find rest, and towards whose protecting power they hasten. So let there be no corruption within it, no treachery, and no deception. Do not enter into any agreement that leaves room for evasion, and do not fall back on the twisting of words once the agreement has been confirmed and made binding. And do not let any hardship that befalls you drive you to break, without right, an agreement God has made binding upon you; for your patience under a difficulty whose relief and good outcome you await is far better than a treachery whose consequence you dread—that a reckoning from God (tilba) should close in upon you, from which you will win no release in this world or the next.

Beware of shedding blood unlawfully, for nothing so invites retribution, weighs heavier in its consequence, or is more apt to end a blessing and cut short a term of life, than the shedding of blood without right. On the Day of Resurrection God—glorified be He—will begin His judgement among His servants with the blood they have shed. So do not seek to strengthen your authority by unlawful bloodshed, for this in truth weakens and undermines it—indeed, it brings it down and takes it away.

You have no excuse before God, nor before me, if you kill anyone deliberately, for that calls forth capital punishment. And if you are overtaken by an error, and your whip or your sword or your hand goes too far in inflicting a penalty—for even a single blow, and what exceeds it, can bring about a death—do not let the pride of your authority hold you back from paying to the relatives of the slain what is rightfully theirs.

Beware of self-admiration, of leaning upon whatever pleases you in yourself, and of loving to be flattered, for these are among the surest openings Satan finds to efface the good works (ihsan) of the virtuous (al-muhsinin). Beware of making your subjects beholden to you for the good you do them, of inflating your own deeds, and of promising them what you then fail to fulfil; for laying people under obligation destroys the good itself, self-praise puts out the light of truth, and breaking a promise earns you the hatred of God and of men. God the Exalted has said: كَبُرَ مَقْتًا عِندَ ٱللَّهِ أَن تَقُولُوا۟ مَا لَا تَفْعَلُونَ — 'It is indeed hateful to God that you say that which you do not do' (61:3). Beware of hastening into matters before their time, and of letting them slip when the moment to act has come; of stubbornness when they grow tangled, and of slackness once they have become plain. Put every matter in its proper place, and do every task in its proper time. Beware of taking for yourself what all men have an equal share in, and of feigning blindness to what is urgent and has become plain to every eye, for this will be taken from you for the benefit of others. Before long the coverings will be lifted from all affairs, and justice will be sought from you on behalf of the wronged. So master the heat of your pride, the sharpness of your temper, the force of your hand, and the edge of your tongue; guard against all of this by holding back the sudden impulse and delaying the blow until your anger has subsided and you regain your self-command. But you will never master this in yourself until you increase your remembrance of your return to your Lord.

It is incumbent upon you to remember what has gone before you of just governance and noble custom, the practice of the Prophet—God bless him and his family—and the obligations laid down in the Book of God; so follow faithfully whatever you have seen us doing in these matters, and strive with all your soul to act by what I have enjoined upon you in this mandate, by which I have made my plea complete against you, so that you may have no excuse when your soul races towards its caprice.

I ask God, by the vastness of His mercy and the greatness of His power to grant every desire, that He grant me and you the success that wins His pleasure—that we may present a clear justification before Him and before His creatures, earn the fairest praise among His servants, and leave behind the most beautiful traces in the land, with blessing made complete and honour ever increased; and that He seal my life and yours with felicity (al-sa'ada) and martyrdom (al-shahada). Truly unto Him we are returning (2:156).

And peace be upon the Messenger of God—God bless him and his good and pure progeny.
